0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.}}}}}}}}}}}}

(17)

Similar to the above, we also have the following equivalent triangulated categories, using the second grading of
\widetilde{\Gamma}
.
Now we have the algebras
\widehat{\Lambda}=\widetilde{\Lambda}\#_{-,*}{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}
with the bound quiver
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
and
\widehat{\Gamma}=\widetilde{\Gamma}\#_{-,*}{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}
with the bound quiver
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{\perp}
.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
\Gamma
be a nicely-graded
n
-slice algebra, and let
\Lambda
be its quadratic dual.
Then the following triangulated categories are equivalent

(9)

the bounded derived category
\mathcal{D}^{b}(\Gamma)
of the finitely generated
\Gamma
-modules;

(10)

the bounded derived category
\mathcal{D}^{b}(\Lambda)
of the finitely generated
\Lambda
-modules;

(11)

the stable category
\underline{\mathrm{grmod}_{-,*}}\widetilde{\Lambda}
of finitely generated graded
\widetilde{\Lambda}
-modules with respect to the second grading;

(12)

the stable category
\underline{\mathrm{grmod}_{-,*}}\Delta(\Gamma)
of finitely generated graded
\Delta(\Gamma)
-modules with respect to the second grading;

(13)

the stable category
\underline{\mathrm{mod}}\widehat{\Lambda}
of finitely generated
\widehat{\Lambda}
-modules;

(14)

the stable category
\underline{\mathrm{mod}}\widehat{\Gamma}
of finitely generated
\widehat{\Gamma}
-modules.

By Theorems
5.3
and
5.4
, we can approach the representation theory of a
n
-slice algebra from different point of views, if it is of infinite type, such approaches also have a non-commutative interpretation as in (8).
In
[
21
]
, the representation theory of
n
-representation-infinite algebra is studied via the category
\mathcal{D}^{b}(\mathrm{qgr}\widetilde{\Gamma})
.

In general,
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{N}
are not well related, see the example 4.7 in
[
14
]
.
The algebra
\Gamma^{N}
is always nicely-graded.
When
\Gamma
is nicely-graded,
\Gamma
is obtained by a sequence of
n
-APR tilts from any indecomposable summand of
\Gamma^{N}
.
So it is natural to study nicely-graded
n
-slice algebras when dealing with the representation theory.

6.
Pairs of higher slice algebras of finite type

Assume that
\Gamma
is an
n
-slice algebra of finite type with Coxeter index
q+1
, it is an
n
-representation-finite algebra with
(q+1,n+1)
-Koszul
(n+1)
-preprojective algebra
\widetilde{\Gamma}
.
Let
\Lambda
be the quadratic dual of
\Gamma
and let
\widetilde{\Lambda}=\Delta_{\sigma}{\Lambda}
be the twisted trivial extension of
\Lambda
.
Let
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1},\rho)
be the bound quiver of
\Lambda
,
Q^{\perp}=(Q_{0},Q_{1},\rho^{\perp})
be the bound quiver of
\Gamma
, then
Q
is an
n
-properly-graded quiver and
Q^{\perp}
is an
n
-slice.
Now we have the returning arrow quiver
\widetilde{Q}
for
\widetilde{\Lambda}
, its quadratic dual quiver
\widetilde{Q}^{\perp}
for
\widetilde{\Gamma}
, the infinite quivers
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\diamond}\widetilde{Q}
for the smash product
\widehat{\Lambda^{N}}=\widetilde{\Lambda}\#_{*,-}{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}
and
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
for the smash product
\widehat{\Lambda}=\widetilde{\Lambda}\#_{-,*}{\mathbb{Z}}^{*}
.

We now show that higher slice algebras of finite type appear in pairs.

Theorem 6.1
.

Assume that
\Gamma
is an acyclic
n
-slice algebra of finite type with Coxeter index
q+1
.
Then there is a
q
-slice algebra
\Gamma^{\prime}
with Coxeter index
n+1
such that their repetitive algebras are quadratic dual.

The
q
-slice algebra
\Gamma^{\prime}
is called a
companion of
\Gamma
.

Proof.

Let
\Gamma
be an acyclic
n
-slice algebra with bound quiver
Q^{\perp}
, and let
\Lambda
be the quadratic dual of
\Gamma
, with bound quiver
Q
.
Then
\widetilde{\Lambda}
and
\widehat{\Lambda}
are both stable
n
-translation algebras with bound quiver the returning arrow quiver
\widetilde{Q}
and the covering quiver
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
, and both are
(n+1,q+1)
-Koszul.
Now consider their respectively quadratic duals
\widetilde{\Gamma}=\widetilde{\Lambda}^{!,op}
and
\widehat{\Gamma}=\widehat{\Lambda}^{!,op}
, their bound quivers are respectively
\widetilde{Q}^{\perp}
and
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{\perp}
.

By Theorem 6.6 of
[
13
]
,
\widetilde{\Lambda}^{!,op}
is the
(n+1)
-preprojective algebra of an
n
-representation-finite algebra
\Gamma
.
So by corollary 3.4 of
[
27
]
,
\widetilde{\Lambda}^{!,op}
is self-injective, and by Proposition 3.4 of
[
3
]
, it is
(q+1,n+1)
-Koszul.
So by Theorem 5.3 of
[
12
]
,
\widehat{\Lambda}^{!,op}
is also
(q+1,n+1)
-Koszul self-injective.
This implies that
\widehat{\Lambda}^{!,op}
is a
q
-translation algebra and
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{\perp}
is a
q
-translation quiver with
q
-translation
\tau_{\perp}
.

Now let
Q^{\prime}
be a complete
\tau_{\perp}
-slice of
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{\perp}
, then it is a
q
-properly-graded quiver by Lemma 6.1 of
[
13
]
and we have that
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{\perp}\simeq{\mathbb{Z}|_{q-1}}Q^{\prime}
by Proposition 3.5 of
[
16
]
.
Let
\Lambda^{\prime}
be the algebra defined by
Q^{\prime}
, it is a
q
-properly-graded algebra.
Similar to the proof of Theorem 6.12 of
[
11
]
,
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{\perp}
is the bound quiver of its repetitive algebra
\widehat{\Lambda^{\prime}}
, which is
(q+1,n+1)
-Koszul, thus
\Gamma^{\prime}={\Lambda^{\prime}}^{!,op}
is an
q
-slice algebra of finite type with Coxeter index
n+1
.

This proves the theorem.
∎

The algebra
\Gamma^{\prime}
is not unique in general, but there are only finite many of them up to isomorphism.
In fact, let
Q^{\prime}
be a complete
\tau_{\perp}
-slice of
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{\perp}
, then any complete
\tau_{\perp}
-slice
Q^{\prime\prime}
of
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{\perp}
has the same number, say
r
, of vertices as
Q^{\prime}
.
Since
\tau_{\perp}
is an automorphism of
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{\perp}
, for any complete
\tau_{\perp}
-slice
Q^{\prime\prime}
of
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{\perp}
, by shifting with
\tau_{\perp}^{t}
if necessary, has a common vertex with
Q^{\prime}
.
Thus for any vertex
i
of
Q^{\prime\prime}
, there is either a path of length no longer than
2r
from some vertex
j
in
Q^{\prime}
to
i
, or a path of length no longer than
2r
from
i
to some vertex
j
in
Q^{\prime}
.
So the vertex set of the non-isomorphic complete
\tau_{\perp}
-slices of
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{\perp}
can be chosen from the set
V
of vertices of the paths of length
2r
starting or ending at a vertex in
Q^{\prime}
, and
V
is a finite set.
This tells us that there are only finite many non-isomorphic complete
\tau_{\perp}
-slices of
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
.

As a corollary of Theorem
6.1
, we have the following result on their bound quivers from its proof.

Corollary 6.2
.

Let
Q^{\perp}
be the bound quiver of an
n
-slice algebra
\Gamma
of finite type with Coxeter index
q+1
and let
{Q^{\prime}}^{\perp}
be the bound quiver of its companion
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Then
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q\simeq{\mathbb{Z}|_{q-1}}{Q^{\prime}}^{\perp}

Now assume that
\Gamma
is nicely-graded and connected, then
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
is a connected component of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\diamond}\widetilde{Q}
, while
{\mathbb{Z}|_{q-1}}Q^{\prime}
is a connected component of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\diamond}\widetilde{Q}^{\prime}
.
Thus the connected components of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\diamond}\widetilde{Q}
and of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\diamond}\widetilde{Q}^{\prime}
are all the same as quivers.

We see from the proof Theorem
6.1
that
\widetilde{Q}^{\perp}
is a
q
-translation quiver with
q
-translation
\tau_{\perp,\widetilde{Q}}
.
Write the
n
-translation of the
n
-translation quiver
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\diamond}\widetilde{Q}
as
\tau
and the
q
-translation of the
q
-translation quiver
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\diamond}\widetilde{Q}^{\perp}
as
\tau_{\perp}
.
With the index of the quiver
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\diamond}\widetilde{Q}
, we have that
\tau(i,s)=(\tau i,s-n-1)
and
\tau_{\perp}(i,s)=(\tau_{\perp,\widetilde{Q}^{\perp}}i,s-q-1)
, by our construction.

Now we study higher translation quivers related to a pair of higher slice algebras of finite type.

A quadratic quiver
\overline{Q}=(\overline{Q}_{0},\overline{Q}_{1},\overline{\rho})
is called a
double translation quiver of type
(n,q)
if
\overline{Q}
is a stable
n
-translation quiver and
\overline{Q}^{\perp}
is a stable
q
-translation quiver.
We obviously have the following proposition, restates Corollary
6.2
.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
\Gamma
be an
n
-slice algebra of finite type with Coxeter index
q+1
with bound quiver
Q^{\perp}
.
Then
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
is a double translation quiver of type
(n,q)
.

If
\overline{Q}
is a double translation quiver of type
(n,q)
, the algebra
\overline{\Lambda}
defined by the bound quiver
\overline{Q}
is an
n
-translation algebra and the algebra
\overline{\Lambda}^{\perp}
defined by the bound quiver
\overline{Q}^{\perp}
is a stable
q
-translation algebra.
So the maximal bound paths of
\overline{Q}
define an
n
-translation
\tau
on
\overline{Q}
and the maximal bound paths of
\overline{Q}^{\perp}
define an
q
-translation
\tau_{\perp}
.
Both defines permutations on the vertex set of
\overline{Q}
, and each of them induces an automorphism on the quiver
\overline{Q}
.
We obviously have the following commutative relation for them.

Lemma 6.4
.

\tau\tau_{\perp}=\tau_{\perp}\tau.

Let
Q
be a complete
\tau
-slice in
\overline{Q}
, then
\overline{Q}={\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
.
So all the double translation quivers of type
(n,q)
are of this form.
As a corollary, we get the following result.

Lemma 6.5
.

If
Q
is a complete
\tau
-slice of
\overline{Q}={\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
, then
\tau_{\perp}^{t}Q
is a complete
\tau
-slice for all the integer
t
.

If
Q^{\prime}
is a complete
\tau_{\perp}
-slice of
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{\perp}
, then
\tau^{t}Q^{\prime}
is a complete
\tau_{\perp}
-slice for all the integer
t
.

As a quiver, we have
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q={\mathbb{Z}|_{q-1}}Q^{\prime}
.

A full convex subquiver
D
of a double translation quiver
\overline{Q}
of type
(n,q)
is call a
double slice
if it is maximal with the property that for any vertex
\bar{i}
in
D
, at most one of
\tau^{-1}\bar{i}
and
\tau_{\perp}\bar{i}
is in
D
.

We remark that if
\bar{i}
,
\tau^{-1}\bar{i}\in D
and there is a bound path of length
q+1
from
\tau^{-1}\bar{i}
to
j
in
\overline{Q}^{\perp}
, then
j\notin D
. If not we have
\tau_{\perp}\tau j=\bar{i}
since
\tau_{\perp}j=\tau^{-1}\bar{i}
and thus
\tau j\in D
, which contradicts the definition of double slice.

We remark that a double slice is an maximal
\tau
-mature bound subquiver, in the term of
\tau
-mature defined in
[
16
]
.

If
\Gamma
is an
n
-slice algebra of Coxeter index
q+1
with bound quiver
Q^{\perp}
, then
{\mathbb{Z}_{\diamond}}\widetilde{Q}
is a double translation quiver of type
(n,q)
, and
{\mathbb{Z}_{\diamond}}\widetilde{Q}[h,h+n+q+1]
is a double slice for any
h
.

Now let
\overline{Q}
be nicely-graded stable double translation quiver of type
(n,q)
, with
n
-translation
\tau
and
q
-translation
\tau_{\perp}
.
If
S
is a complete
\tau
-slice in
\overline{Q}
, let
D(S+)
be the full subquiver formed by
S
and all the
\tau_{\perp}
-hammocks
H^{{\bar{i}}}
starting at each vertex
{\bar{i}}
of
S
, let
D(-S)
be the full subquiver formed by
S
and all the
\tau_{\perp}
-hammocks
H_{{\bar{i}}}
ending at each vertex
{\bar{i}}
of
S
.

In
D(S+)
, we have two complete
\tau
-slices,
S
and
\tau_{\perp}^{-1}S
, and in
D(-S)
, we have two
\tau
-slices,
S
and
\tau_{\perp}S
.
They are the union of
\tau_{\perp}
-hammocks connecting the vertices these two
\tau
-slices.
Note that each
\tau
-hammock is convex, so they are also convex.

Proposition 6.6
.

D(S+)
and
D(-S)
are convex.

We clearly have that
D(S+)=D(-\tau_{\perp}^{-1}S)
.

Write
-S_{\perp}
for the full subquiver of
D(S+)
with vertex set
D(S+)_{0}\setminus S_{0}
, and write
S_{\perp}+
for the full subquiver of
D(-S)
with vertex set
D(-S)_{0}\setminus S_{0}
.

Proposition 6.7
.

The full subquiver
D(S+)
is formed by a complete
\tau
-slice
S
, and a
\tau_{\perp}
-slice
-S_{\perp}
, connected with arrows from non-source vertices of
S
to non-sink vertices
-S_{\perp}
.

The full subquiver
D(-S)
is formed by a complete
\tau
-slice
S
, and a complete
\tau_{\perp}
-slice
S_{\perp}+
, connected with arrows from non-source vertices of
S_{\perp}+
to non-sink vertices
S
.

Proof.

We prove the first assertion, the second follows dually.

We prove that
-S_{\perp}
forms a
\tau_{\perp}
-slice in
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{\perp}
.
If there is a vertex
{\bar{j}}
such that the
\tau_{\perp}
-orbit of
{\bar{j}}
has no intersection with
-S_{\perp}
, we may choose
{\bar{j}}
such that there is an arrow
\alpha:{\bar{j}}\to{\bar{i}}
such that
{\bar{i}}
is in
-S_{\perp}
.
But this force
{\bar{j}}
in
S
, so
\tau_{\perp}^{-1}{\bar{j}}
is in
-S_{\perp}
, this is a contradiction.
So
-S_{\perp}
intersect each
\tau_{\perp}
-orbit by the construction,
-S_{\perp}
intersects each
\tau_{\perp}
-orbit at most once.

Now
-S_{\perp}
is obtained as a union of
\tau_{\perp}
-hammocks with their sources removed, it is convex since each of these source removed
\tau_{\perp}
-hammocks are convex, and all the removed sources form a convex set which preceding
-S_{\perp}
.

This proves that
-S_{\perp}
forms a
\tau_{\perp}
-slice in
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{\perp}
.
∎

Proposition 6.8
.

D(S+)
and
D(-S)
are double slices.

Proof.

We prove that
D(S+)
is a double slice.
The other assertion follows from duality.

For each vertex
{\bar{i}}
in
D(S+)
, if
{\bar{i}}
and
\tau_{\perp}^{-1}{\bar{i}}
are in
D(S+)
, then by the construction
{\bar{i}}
is in
S
, so
\tau{\bar{i}}
is not
S
and hence not in
D(S+)
.

Now
\tau_{\perp}^{-1}S
also forms a complete
\tau
-slice isomorphic to
S
.
If
{\bar{j}}
is a vertex not in
D(S+)
such that there is an arrow from
{\bar{j}}
to a vertex
{\bar{i}}^{\prime}
in
S
, then we have
\tau^{-1}{\bar{j}}
is in
S
and
\tau_{\perp}^{-1}\tau^{-1}{\bar{j}}
in
D(S+)
.
If
{\bar{j}}
is a vertex not in
D(S+)
such that there is an arrow from
{\bar{i}}^{\prime}
in
\tau_{\perp}^{-1}S
to
{\bar{j}}
, then we have
\tau{\bar{j}}
in
\tau_{\perp}^{-1}S
and
\tau_{\perp}\tau{\bar{j}}
in
S
.

This shows that
D(S+)
is maximal, and hence a double slice.
∎

Note that the vertex set of
\tau(-S_{\perp})
is also in
D(S)
and it is a complete
\tau_{\perp}
-slice in
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{\perp}
.
Regard as a double translation quiver of type
(q,n)
, we also construct the double slice
D(\tau(-S_{\perp}))
and
D(\tau^{-1}(S_{\perp}+))
in
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{\perp}
, and obviously have the following.

Proposition 6.9
.

D(\tau(-S_{\perp}))=D(S+)^{\perp}
, and
D(\tau^{-1}(S_{\perp}+))=D(-S)^{\perp}
.

Let
Q^{\perp}
be the bound quiver of the
n
-slice algebra
R
, then
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
is a double translation quiver of type
(n,q)
, and
S=Q
is a
\tau
-slice.
The algebra
R^{-,\perp}
defined by
\tau(-S_{\perp})
and
R^{+,\perp}
defined by
\tau^{-1}(S_{\perp}+)
are companions of
R
.
Called the
right
and
left
companion of
R
, respectively.
We usually take the right companion of
R
as default one and write it as
\Gamma^{c}
.

Recall that the complete
\tau
-slices are related by the
\tau
-mutations defined as follow.
If
i
is a sink of a complete
\tau
-slice
S
, we define the
\tau
-mutation

s_{i}^{-}S
of
S
at
i
as the full bound subquiver of
\overline{Q}
obtained by replacing the vertex
i
by its
n
-translation
\tau i
.
If
i
is a source of a complete
\tau
-slices
S
, define the
\tau
-mutation

s_{i}^{+}S
of
S
at
i
as the full bound subquiver in
\overline{Q}
obtained by replacing the vertex
i
by its inverse
n
-translation
\tau^{-1}i
.

Double slices are also related by mutations.

The mutation
s^{i}D
of double slice
D
with respect to a source
i
is obtained by removing the vertex
i
and add the vertex
\tau_{\perp}^{-1}\tau^{-1}i
and the arrows to
\tau_{\perp}^{-1}\tau^{-1}i
in
\overline{Q}
;
the mutation
s_{i}D
of double slice
D
with respect to a sink
i
is obtained by removing the vertex
i
and add the vertex
\tau\tau_{\perp}i
and the arrows from
\tau\tau_{\perp}i
in
\overline{Q}
.

Proposition 6.10
.

The
\tau
-mutation
s^{{\bar{i}}}
on
S
induces a mutation
s^{{\bar{i}}}
on
D(S+)
; The
\tau
-mutation
s_{{\bar{i}}}
on
S
induces a mutation
s_{{\bar{i}}}
on
D(-S)
.

Proof.

We prove the first assertion, the second follows from duality.

By apply
\tau
-mutation
s^{{\bar{i}}}
on
S
to get
s^{{\bar{i}}}S
, we remove the source
{\bar{i}}
from
S
and add
\tau^{-1}{\bar{i}}
as a sink of
s^{{\bar{i}}}S
.

Since
n,q\geq 2
, if there are arrow
{\bar{i}}\to{\bar{j}}_{s}
,
s=1,\cdots,h
for some
h
, then
{\bar{j}}_{s}
is in
S
and all the vertices of the
\tau_{\perp}
-hammocks
H^{{\bar{i}}}
except
{\bar{i}}
is contained the union of these
H^{{\bar{j}}_{s}}
.
Similarly, for the arrows
{\bar{j}}^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}\to\tau^{-1}{\bar{i}}
, all the vertices of the
\tau_{\perp}
-hammocks
H^{\tau^{-1}{\bar{i}}}
except
\tau^{-1}_{\perp}\tau^{-1}{\bar{i}}
are contained the union of these
H^{{\bar{j}}^{\prime}_{s^{\prime}}}
.

This proves
s^{i}D(S+)=D(s^{i}S+)
.
∎

By Lemma 6.5 of
[
11
]
and the above Proposition, we have the following result.

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
D
and
D^{\prime}
be two double slice of a stable double translation quiver.
Then there is a sequences of mutations
s_{1},\cdots,s_{l}
such that
s_{l}\cdots s_{1}D=D^{\prime}
.

Now assume that
Q
is nicely-graded and connected, then
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
and
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{\perp}
are both nicely-graded and connected.
So these quivers are connected component of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\diamond}\widetilde{Q}
.
Using the index of the vertices of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\diamond}\widetilde{Q}
for the vertices of
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
and
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{\perp}
.
Now for any
t
,
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\diamond}\widetilde{Q}[t,t+q+n+1]
is a double slice of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\diamond}\widetilde{Q}
, and we can take
D^{0}={\mathbb{Z}}_{\diamond}\widetilde{Q}[-n,q+1]
, then any double slice can be obtained from
D^{0}
by a sequences of mutations by Proposition
6.11
.

7.
The higher preprojective components

Now we study the representations of the pair of higher slice algebras of finite type, showing that they share the same quiver as the Auslander-Reiten quiver for their higher preprojective components.

For a finite dimensional algebra
\Gamma
of global dimension
n
, the
n
-Auslander-Reiten translations of
\Gamma
modules are introduced by Iyama (see
[
22
,
23
]
),

\tau_{n}^{-1}=\mathrm{Tr}\,D\Omega^{1-n}=\mathrm{Ext}_{\Gamma}^{n}(D-,\Gamma)\quad and\quad\tau_{n}=D\mathrm{Tr}\,\Omega^{n-1}=D\mathrm{Ext}_{\Gamma}^{n}(-,\Gamma),

with the convention that
\tau_{n}^{0}=\tau_{n}^{-0}=\mathds{1}
.

Modules in

\mathcal{M}^{+}(D\Gamma)=\mathrm{add}\,\{\tau_{n}^{t}D\Gamma|t\geq 0\}\mbox{ and }\mathcal{M}^{-}(\Gamma)=\mathrm{add}\,\{\tau_{n}^{-t}\Gamma|t\geq 0\}

are called
n
-preinjective modules of
\Gamma
and
n
-preprojective modules of
\Gamma
, respectively (see
[
21
]
).
These modules are natural generalization of preinjective and preprojective modules, respectively, thus these classes are called
n
-preprojective component
and
n
-preinjective component
, respectively.

Now assume that
\Gamma
is an acyclic
n
-slice algebra of finite type with bound quiver
Q^{\perp}
, then
\Gamma
is
n
-representation-finite.
By Proposition 1.12 of
[
25
]
, we have that
\mathcal{M}^{+}(D\Gamma)=\mathcal{M}^{-}(\Gamma)
, which we write as
\mathcal{M}(\Gamma)
.
By Proposition 2.3 of
[
26
]
,
\mathcal{M}(\Gamma)
is an
n
-cluster tilting subcategory and by Theorem 3.3.1 of
[
22
]
,
\mathcal{M}(\Gamma)
has
n
-almost split sequences.

Assume that
\Gamma
is nicely-graded and connected, so
Q
is nicely-graded connected quiver and each connected components of
{\mathbb{Z}_{\diamond}}\widetilde{Q}
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
, which is an
n
-translation quiver, write its
n
-translation as
\tau
.

Let
\overline{\Lambda}
be the algebra defined by the bound quiver
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
and let
\overline{\Gamma}
be the algebra defined by the bound quiver
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{\perp}
.
Since
\widetilde{\Lambda}
is an
n
-translation algebra,
\overline{\Lambda}
is an
n
-translation algebra with Coxeter index
q+1
, by Theorem 5.3 of
[
12
]
.
So
\overline{\Gamma}
is
(q+1,n+1)
-Koszul.
By Corollary
6.2
,
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{\perp}
is a
q
-translation quiver, write
\tau_{\perp}
for its
q
-translation.
Identify
Q
with the complete
\tau
-slice
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q[0]
of
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
and write
i
for the vertex
(i,0)
in
Q
when needed.

Let
d
be a function
d:{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q\to{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
d(j,t^{\prime})=d(i,t)+1
if there is an arrow from
(i,t)
to
(j,t^{\prime})
, we may assume that
\mathrm{max}\{d(i)|i\in Q_{0}\}=0
.
Fix a vertex
(i,t)
in
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
, set

\mathfrak{d}_{(i,t)}(j,t^{\prime})=(t-t^{\prime})(n+1)+d(i)-d(j).

Since
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
is nicely-graded, a
\tau
-hammock
H_{(i,t)}

[
11
,
16
]
in
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
ending at a vertex
(i,t)
is identified with the full subquiver of
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
with vertex set

H_{(i,t),0}=\left\{(j,t^{\prime})\mid(j,t^{\prime})\in{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q_{0},\exists\mbox{ path }0\neq p\in\overline{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{d}_{(i,t)}(j,t^{\prime})},s(p)=(j,t^{\prime}),t(p)=(i,t)\right\}.

The hammock function
\mu_{(i,t)}:H_{(i,t),0}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{Z}}
is an integral map on the vertices defined by

\mu_{(i,t)}(j,t^{\prime})=\mathrm{dim}\,_{k}e_{(i,t)}\overline{\Lambda}_{\mathfrak{d}_{(i,t)}(j,t^{\prime})}e_{(j,t^{\prime})}.

For each
(i,t)
in
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
, we have a Koszul complex

\mathbf{M}_{(i,t)}:M_{n+1}=\overline{\Gamma}e_{(i,t)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{n+1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\to M_{r}\to\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M_{0}=\overline{\Gamma}e_{(i,t-1)}

(18)

in
\mathrm{add}\,\overline{\Gamma}
, with

M_{r}=\bigoplus_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(j,t^{\prime})\in H_{(i,t),0}}}{{\mathfrak{d}_{(i,t)}(j,t^{\prime})=n+1-r}}}(\overline{\Gamma}e_{(j,t^{\prime})})^{\mu_{(i,t)}(j,t^{\prime})}

for
0\leq r\leq n+1
.
This is the projective resolution of the simple
\overline{\Gamma}
-module
\overline{\Gamma}_{0}e_{(i,t-1)}
(Proposition 2.1 of
[
16
]
, see also Proposition 7.4 of
[
12
]
).
Since
\overline{\Gamma}
is
(q+1,n+1)
-Koszul algebra, we have that

\mathrm{Ker}\,f_{n+1}\simeq\overline{\Gamma}_{0}e_{\tau_{\perp}^{-1}(i,t)},

the simple
\overline{\Gamma}
-module
\overline{\Gamma}e_{\tau_{\perp}^{-1}(i,t)}
corresponding to the vertex
\tau_{\perp}^{-1}(i,t)
.

It is obvious that, regarding as a bound quiver, each convex full subquiver
T
of
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
is an
n
-translation quiver and it defines an
n
-translation algebra
\Lambda(T)
with Coxeter number
q+1
(so it is an
(n+1,q+1)
-algebra).
Write its quadratic dual as
\Gamma(T)
.
Then

\Lambda(T)=e_{T}\overline{\Lambda}e_{T}\mbox{ and }\Gamma(T)=e_{T}\overline{\Gamma}e_{T}

for the idempotent
e_{T}=\sum_{(i,t)\in T_{0}}e_{(i,t)}
.
Let
T
be a convex full subquiver with
Q
a terminal complete
\tau
-slice, then
\mathrm{add}\,\Gamma
is full subcategory of

\mathcal{G}(T)=\mathrm{add}\,\Gamma(T)\simeq\mathrm{add}\,\{\overline{\Gamma}e_{(i,t)}\mid(i,t)\in T_{0}\}.

By Lemma 7.1 of
[
12
]
, we have the following Lemma.

Lemma 7.1
.

If
H_{(i,t)}
is in
T
, then
e_{T}\mathbf{M}_{(i,t)}\,
is an
n
-almost split sequence in
\mathcal{G}(T)
if and only if
\mathrm{Ker}\,f_{n+1}|_{T}=0
.

Let
D=D(-Q)
be the double slice with
Q
a terminal complete
\tau
-slice, then
D^{op}
is a double slice in
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{op}
.
So we have that
Q^{op}
is embedded into
D^{op}
as an initial complete
\tau
-slice.

Let
\mathcal{G}(D)
be the bound path category defined by the bound quiver
D
with relation set the restriction of
\rho^{\perp}_{{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q}
.
We have that

\mathcal{G}(D)\simeq\mathrm{add}\,\{\overline{\Gamma}e_{(j,r)}|(j,r)\in D_{0}\}\simeq\mathrm{add}\,\{e_{D}\overline{\Gamma}e_{(j,r)}|(j,r)\in D_{0}\}

by Proposition 2.6 of
[
16
]
.
Since
D
is a double slice, if
(i,-t)
and
(i,-t-1)
, are in
D
, then
\tau^{-1}_{\perp}(i,-t)
is not in
D
and
\mathrm{Ker}\,f_{n+1}\mid_{D}=0
in
e_{D}\mathbf{M}_{(i,-t)}\,
, by Lemma
7.1
and Theorem 7.5 of
[
12
]
, we have the following Lemma.

Lemma 7.2
.

\mathcal{G}(D)
has
n
-almost split sequence.

If both
(i,-t)
and
(i,-t-1)
are in
D
, then
e_{D}\mathbf{M}_{(i,-t)}\,
(
18
) is an
n
-almost split sequence in
\mathcal{G}(D)
.

Let
e=e_{Q}
, then
\Gamma=e\overline{\Gamma}e
.
Write
Q(\mathcal{M}(\Gamma))
for the full subquiver of
{\mathbb{Z}}|_{n-1}Q^{op}
with vertex set

Q_{0}(\mathcal{M}(\Gamma))=\{(j,-r)\in{\mathbb{Z}}|_{n-1}Q^{op}_{0}\quad|\tau_{n}^{-r}\Gamma e_{j}\neq 0\}

for
j\in Q^{op}_{0}
.

Lemma 7.3
.

The bound subquiver
Q(\mathcal{P}(\Gamma))
of
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q^{op}
is convex.

Proof.

Write
Q(\mathcal{P}(\Gamma))=Q
for the full subquiver of
Q(\mathcal{M}(\Gamma))
with vertex set

Q_{0}(\mathcal{P}(\Gamma))=\{(j,0)\in{\mathbb{Z}}|_{n-1}Q^{op}_{0}\quad|j\in Q^{op}_{0}\}

and
Q(\mathcal{I}(\Gamma))
for the full subquiver of
Q(\mathcal{M}(\Gamma))
with vertex set

Q_{0}(\mathcal{I}(\Gamma))=\{(j,-r)\in Q_{0}(\mathcal{M}(\Gamma))\quad|\tau_{n}^{-r-1}\Gamma e_{j}=0\}.

By Proposition 1.12 of
[
25
]
,
Q(\mathcal{P}(\Gamma))
and
Q(\mathcal{I}(\Gamma))
are isomorphic, and they form complete
\tau
-slices. By definition, the vertices in
Q(\mathcal{M}(\Gamma))
are exactly vertices lying between
Q(\mathcal{P}(\Gamma))
and
Q(\mathcal{I}(\Gamma))
, so
Q(\mathcal{M}(\Gamma))
is convex.
∎

The vertices of
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
is totally ordered such that
(i,t)<(j,t^{\prime})
if
t<t^{\prime}
, or
t=t^{\prime}
and
d(i)<d(j)
.
For a convex subquiver
T
, write
T(i,t)
the subquiver of
T
consisting of the vertives
(j,t^{\prime})
with
(j,t^{\prime})>(i,t)
, and
T[i,t]
the subquiver of
T
consisting of the vertives
(j,t^{\prime})
with
(j,t^{\prime})\geq(i,t)
.
Let
(i_{0},0)
be the element in
Q
which is maximal with respect to this order.

Now we show that the subquivers
Q(\mathcal{M}(\Gamma))
and
D^{op}
are the same.

Lemma 7.4
.

D^{op}=Q(\mathcal{M}(\Gamma)

Proof.

We prove that they have the same vertex set, that is
D_{0}=Q_{0}(\mathcal{M}(\Gamma))
.

Note that
\mathcal{G}(D)
is an Orlov category, so
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{G}(D)}(\overline{\Gamma}e_{(i,-t)},\overline{\Gamma}e_{(j,-t^{\prime})}=0
whenever
(j,-t^{\prime})>(i,-t)
.
By Proposition 5.3 of
[
16
]
,
\mathcal{M}(\Gamma)
is an Orlov category, with respect to the order defined above.
Set

\mathcal{G}(D)(j,-r)=\mathrm{add}\,\{\overline{\Gamma}e_{(i,-t)}\mid(i,-t)\in D_{0}(j,-r)\},

\mathcal{G}(D)[j,-r]=\mathrm{add}\,\{\overline{\Gamma}e_{(i,-t)}\mid(i,-t)\in D_{0}[j,-r]\},

\mathcal{M}(\Gamma)(j,-r)=\mathrm{add}\,\{\tau_{n}^{-t}\Gamma e_{i}\mid(i,-t)\in Q_{0}(\mathcal{M}(\Gamma))(j,-r)\}

and

\mathcal{M}(\Gamma)[j,-r]=\mathrm{add}\,\{\tau_{n}^{-t}\Gamma e_{i}\mid(i,-t)\in Q_{0}(\mathcal{M}(\Gamma))[j,-r]\}.

Then

D^{op}(i_{0},-1)=Q^{op}=Q(\mathcal{M}(\Gamma))(i_{0},-1),

\mathcal{G}(D)(i_{0},-1)\simeq\mathcal{M}(\Gamma)(i_{0},-1)\simeq\mathrm{add}\,\Gamma,

and we have for each
j\in Q_{0}
,

e\overline{\Gamma}e_{(j,0)}=\Gamma e_{j}.

Now assume for a vertex
(i,-r-1)
,

\mathcal{G}(D)(i,-r-1)\simeq\mathcal{M}(\Gamma)(i,-r-1)

and for each
(j,-t)\in D_{0}(i,-r-1)
,

e\overline{\Gamma}e_{(j,-t)}\simeq\tau_{n}^{-t}\Gamma e_{j}.

If
(i,-r),(i,-r-1)\in D_{0}
, then
e_{D}\mathbf{M}_{(i,-r)}\,
is an
n
-almost split sequence in
\mathcal{G}(D)
, by Lemma
7.2
.
So
e\mathbf{M}_{(i,-r)}\,
is a sink sequence in
\mathcal{G}(D)[i,-r-1]
, hence also a sink sequence in
\mathcal{M}(\Gamma)[i,-r-1]
.
Since
\mathcal{M}(\Gamma)
is Orlov, it is a sink sequence in
\mathcal{M}(\Gamma)
.
By Proposition 3.3 of
[
22
]
,
e\mathbf{M}_{(i,-r)}\,
is an
n
-almost split sequence in
\mathcal{M}(\Gamma)
and by Theorem 3.3.1 of
[
22
]
,

e\overline{\Gamma}e_{(i,-r-1)}\simeq\tau_{n}^{-1}e\overline{\Gamma}e_{(i,-r)}\simeq\tau_{n}^{-r-1}\Gamma e_{i}.

So
\mathcal{G}(D)[i,-r-1]
is equivalent to
\mathcal{M}(\Gamma)[i,-r-1]
.
Inductively, we see

D_{0}\subset Q_{0}(\mathcal{M}(\Gamma))

and

\mathcal{G}(D)\simeq\mathrm{add}\,\{\overline{\Gamma}e_{(i,-t)}\mid(i,-t)\in D_{0}\}

is regarded as a full subcategory of

\mathcal{M}(\Gamma)\simeq\mathrm{add}\,\{\overline{\Gamma}e_{(i,-t)}\mid(i,-t)\in Q_{0}(\mathcal{M}(\Gamma))\}.

If
(i,-r-1)\not\in D_{0}
, we have both
\tau^{-1}(i,-r-1)=(i,-r)
and
\tau_{\perp}(i,-r)=(i^{\prime},-t^{\prime})
are in
D
, since
D
is double slice.

Since
\mathbf{M}_{(i,-r)}\,
is a sink sequence in
\mathrm{add}\,\overline{\Gamma}
, it is a sink sequence in
\mathrm{add}\,\{\mathcal{G}(D),\overline{\Gamma}e_{(i,-r-1)}\}
, since
\mathrm{add}\,\overline{\Gamma}
is Orlov.

If
(i,-r-1)\in Q_{0}(\mathcal{M}(\Gamma))
then
\tau_{n}^{-r-1}\Gamma e_{i}\neq 0
,
e\mathbf{M}_{(i,-r)}\,
is a sink sequemce and hence an
n
-almost split sequence in
\mathcal{M}(\Gamma)
, by Proposition 3.3 of
[
22
]
and

e\overline{\Gamma}e_{(i,-r-1)}\simeq\tau_{n}^{-r-1}\Gamma e_{i}.

So
(e_{D}+e_{(i,-r-1)})\mathbf{M}_{(i,-r)}\,
is also a source sequence in
\mathcal{G}(D\cup(i,-r-1))
, where
D\cup(i,-r-1)
is the full bound subquiver of
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
with vertex set
D_{0}\cup\{(i,-r-1)\}
.
But as the complex of
\Gamma(D\cup(i,-r-1))
,
\mathrm{Ker}\,f_{n+1}\mid_{\Gamma(D\cup(i,-r-1))}\simeq\overline{\Gamma}_{0}e_{\tau_{\perp}(i,-r)}
which is not zero since
\tau_{\perp}(i,-r)
is in
D
.
This contradicts Lemma
7.1
.

So
(i,-r-1)\not\in Q_{0}(\mathcal{M}(\Gamma))
.
This proves
D^{op}=Q(\mathcal{M}(\Gamma)
.
∎

As a corollary of Lemma
7.4
, we obtain the following theorem.

Lemma 7.5
.

We have the equivalence

\mathcal{G}(D)\simeq\mathcal{M}({\Gamma})
.

Remark
. From the proof of Lemma
7.5
, we see the assumption of “
\tau
-mature” in Theorem 5.6 of
[
16
]
can be removed.

As an immediate consequence of Lemma
7.5
, we have the following result on the Auslander-Reiten quiver of
n
-slice algebra of finite type.

Theorem 7.6
.

Let
\Gamma
be an
n
-slice algebra of finite type with nicely-graded bound quiver
Q^{\perp}
.
Then the Auslander-Reiten quiver of its
n
-preprojective component of
\Gamma
is the opposite quiver of a double slice in
{\mathbb{Z}|_{n-1}}Q
.

More precise, we have

Theorem 7.7
.

Let
\Gamma
be an
n
-slice algebra of finite type with nicely-graded bound quiver
Q^{\perp}
and
q+1
as its Coxeter index and let
\mathcal{Q}
be the Auslander-Reiten quiver of its
n
-preprojective modules.
Then

(1)

There is a
q
-slice algebra
\Gamma^{c}
of finite type such that
\mathcal{Q}
is the Auslander-Reiten quiver of the
q
-preprojective component of
\Gamma^{c}
.

(2)

\mathcal{Q}=D(Q)^{op}
.

(3)

As a quiver
\mathcal{Q}
is the opposite quiver of a quiver obtained by connecting the quiver
Q^{\perp}
of
\Gamma
and the quiver
{Q^{c}}^{\perp}
of
\Gamma^{c}
by some arrows.

Note that the category of
n
-preprojective modules of a
n
-representation-finite algebra is an
n
-cluster tilted subcategory Proposition 2.6 of
[
21
]
.
We have the following result.

Theorem 7.8
.

Let
\Gamma
be an
n
-slice algebra of finite type with Coxeter index
q+1
, and let
\Gamma^{c}
be its companion.
Then the
n
-cluster tilted subcategory of
\Gamma
and the
q
-cluster tilted subcategory of
\Gamma^{c}
are quadratic duals.

As a corollaries, we have the following results for hereditary algebras, using Corollary 4.3 of
[
3
]
.

Corollary 7.9
.

Let
\Gamma
be an hereditary algebra of finite type and let
h
be Coxeter number of its quiver.
Then there is an
(h-3)
-slice algebra
\Gamma^{c}
of finite type such that the module category of
\Gamma
is the quadratic dual of the
(h-3)
-cluster tilted subcategory of
\Gamma^{c}
-modules.

Using the higher Auslander algebra introduced in
[
24
]
, one gets the following Corollary.

Corollary 7.10
.

For each
n
-Auslander algebra
{\mathcal{A}}
of an n-slice algebra of finite type, there exist a
q
and a
q
-Auslander algebra
{\mathcal{A}}^{c}
of a q-slice algebra of finite type such that
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{c}
are quadratic dual one another.

It is interesting to know if Theorem
7.8
holds for higher cluster tilting subcategories of finite type in general?

Higher slice algebras of finite type appear in pairs, the following is an example.

Example 7.11
.

The following example was presented in
[
18
]
to illustrates the
\tau
-hammocks and
\tau
-mutations for
n
-slice algebra of finite type.
Now we consider its dual higher slice algebra.
The Auslander algebra
\Gamma
of the path algebra of type
A_{3}
with linear orientation, is a
2
-representation-finite algebra, given by the quiver
Q
:

\textstyle{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 6}}{{\circ}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 5}}{{\circ}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 3}}{{\circ}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 4}}{{\circ}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 2}}{{\circ}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{\circ}}}

with
\alpha_{i}
for the vertical arrow ending at vertex
i
, and
\beta
for the horizontal arrow ending at vertex
i
.
The the relation set
\rho=\{\alpha\alpha,\beta\beta,\alpha\beta-\beta\alpha\}
and
\rho^{\perp}=\{\alpha\beta+\beta\alpha\}\cup\{\alpha_{1}\beta_{2},\alpha_{4}\beta_{5}\}
.
The returning arrow quiver
\widetilde{Q}
,

0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

is obtained from
Q
by adding the arrow
\gamma_{i}
going up left to vertex
i
.
The relation set
\tilde{\rho}=\{\alpha\alpha,\beta\beta,\gamma\gamma,\alpha\beta-\beta\alpha,\alpha\gamma-\gamma\alpha,\beta\gamma-\gamma\beta\}
, and its dual relation set
\tilde{\rho}^{\perp}=\{\alpha\beta+\beta\alpha,\alpha\gamma+\gamma\alpha,\beta\gamma+\gamma\beta\}\cup\{\alpha_{1}\beta_{2},\alpha_{4}\beta_{5},\beta_{2}\gamma_{4},\beta_{3}\gamma_{5},\gamma_{5}\alpha_{2},\gamma_{6}\alpha_{4}\}
.
For each rhombus formed by two paths of length
2
, we have commutative relations in both relation sets.
We have zero relations for paths of length
2
of the same type (going in the same direction in our presentation) in
\tilde{\rho}
, while zero relations in
\tilde{\rho}^{\perp}
are the complements in the cyclic paths of length 3 of the arrows
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\beta_{3},\beta_{5},\gamma_{4},\gamma_{6}
.

With the relation set
\tilde{\rho}
,
\widetilde{Q}
becomes a
2
-translation quiver with the trivial translation, and with quadratic dual relation
\tilde{\rho}^{\perp}
,
\widetilde{Q}
becomes a
1
-translation quiver with translation defined by
\tau 1=3,\tau 2=5,\tau 3=6,\tau 4=2,\tau 5=4,\tau 6=1
.

The
3
connected components of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{\diamond}\widetilde{Q}
look the same, they are isomorphic to the quiver
{\mathbb{Z}|_{1}}Q
as follows.

They all produce isomorphic algebras.
Other
2
-slice quivers can be obtained by taking
\tau
-mutations on homogeneous ones with
S_{4}=s^{(2,0)}S_{1}
,
S_{5}=s^{(1,0)}S_{2}
and
S_{5}=s^{(1,1)}s^{(2,0)}S_{1}
.

They are isomorphic
1
-slices in
\overline{Q}
.
Using
\tau_{\perp}
-mutation, we get all the
1
-slice quivers in
\overline{Q}
.
The non-isomorphic ones are
T_{4}=s^{(6,0)}T_{1}
,
T_{5}=s^{(2,0)}T_{1}
and
T_{6}=s^{(1,1)}s^{(2,0)}T_{1}
.
